#### **Religion**

#### **Christianity**

The religious demographics of Illinois closely mirror the religious demography of the nation as a whole. [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christians) of any denomination make up 62% of the population of Illinois, a share identical to the estimated national percentage of 62%, according to the Pew Research Center. [\[201\]](#page-37-15)

However, looking at specific denominations, Illinois has a significantly larger Catholic population than most states. Roman Catholics constitute the single largest religious denomination in Illinois; they are heavily concentrated in and around Chicago, reflecting the prominent Hispanic, Polish, Irish, and Italian diasporas in the area.[\[202\]](#page-37-16) As of 2023, Catholics account for nearly 25% of the state's population. In 2010, Catholics in Illinois numbered 3,648,907, while by 2020, this number had declined to 3,099,544.[\[202\]](#page-37-16) The first and only American-born Catholic [pope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope), [Pope Leo XIV,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Leo_XIV) was born in Chicago and raised in the suburb of [Dolton.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolton,_Illinois)

When taken together as a group, the various Protestant denominations comprise a greater percentage of the state's population than do Catholics, making up 36% of the state's population. The largest Protestant denominations in 2020 were the [United Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Methodist_Church) [Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Methodist_Church) with 235,045 members and the [Southern Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention) with 222,589. Illinois has one of the largest concentrations of [Missouri Synod Lutherans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Synod_Lutherans) in the United States, at 182,097.

Illinois played an important role in the early [Latter Day Saint movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latter_Day_Saint_movement), with [Nauvoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nauvoo,_Illinois) becoming a gathering place for Mormons in the early 1840s. Following the 1844 [killing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Joseph_Smith)

## ![](_page_18_Figure_6.jpeg)

[of Mormon leader Joseph Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Joseph_Smith) by a lynch mob in nearby Carthage, Nauvoo was the location of the [succession crisis,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_crisis_(Latter_Day_Saints)) which led to the separation of the Mormon movement into [several Latter Day Saint sects.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_denominations_in_the_Latter_Day_Saint_movement) [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints) the largest of the sects to emerge from the Mormon schism, has more than 55,000 adherents in Illinois today. [\[203\]](#page-37-17)

#### **Other Abrahamic religious communities**

## ![](_page_18_Picture_9.jpeg)

The Bahá'í House of [Worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_House_of_Worship_(Wilmette,_Illinois)) in [Wilmette,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmette,_Illinois) Illinois

A significant number of adherents of other [Abrahamic faiths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions) can be found in Illinois. Largely concentrated in the [Chicago metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area), followers of the [Muslim,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) [Bahá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith)'í, and [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews) religions all call the state home.[\[204\]](#page-38-0) Muslims constituted the largest non-Christian group, with 473,792 adherents. Illinois has the largest concentration of Muslims by state in the country, with 3.7% of the population being Muslim.[\[205\]](#page-38-1) The highest concentration of Muslims in the state is in suburban [Du Page county,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Du Page_County,_Illinois) where they make up approximately 7.6% of the population.[\[206\]](#page-38-2) In the Chicago area as a whole, around 4.7% of the population are Muslim, the highest rate of any major metropolitan area in the United States.[\[205\]](#page-38-1)

The largest and oldest surviving Bahá'[í House of Worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_House_of_Worship_(Wilmette,_Illinois)) in the world is located on the shores of [Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan) [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan) in [Wilmette, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmette,_Illinois), one of eight continental Bahá'[í House of Worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_House_of_Worship). [\[207\]](#page-38-3) It serves as a space for people of all backgrounds and religions to gather, meditate, reflect, and pray, expressing the [Bahá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith)'í principle of the [oneness of religions.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith_and_the_unity_of_religion) [\[208\]](#page-38-4)

The Chicago area has a very large Jewish community, particularly in the northern suburbs of [Skokie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skokie,_Illinois), [Buffalo Grove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffalo_Grove,_Illinois), [Highland Park,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Park,_Illinois) and surrounding areas. Former Chicago Mayor [Rahm Emanuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahm_Emanuel) was the Windy City's first Jewish mayor. The current governor of Illinois, [J. B. Pritzker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JB_Pritzker), is Jewish, being the third person of Jewish descent to hold the office after [Henry Horner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Horner) and [Samuel H. Shapiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Shapiro_(Illinois_politician)). [\[209\]](#page-38-5) Almost 4% of the Chicago area's population is Jewish, numbering over 300,000 people.[\[210\]](#page-38-6)

#### **Other religions**

Chicago is also home to a significant population of [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), [Sikhs,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh) [Jains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism), and [Buddhists.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism) [\[204\]](#page-38-0)

# **Economy**

As of 2022, the [gross state product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U. S._states_by_GDP_(nominal)) for Illinois reached US\$1.0 trillion.[\[211\]](#page-38-7)

As of February 2019, the unemployment rate in Illinois reached 4.2%.[\[212\]](#page-38-8)

Illinois's [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage_in_the_United_States) will rise to \$15 per hour by 2025, making it one of the highest in the nation.[\[213\]](#page-38-9)

#### **Agriculture**

Illinois's major agricultural outputs are [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), [soybeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean), [hogs,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pig) [cattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle), [dairy products,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dairy_product) and wheat. In most years, Illinois is either the first or second state for the highest production of soybeans, with a harvest of 427.7 million bushels (11.64 million [metric tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonne)) in 2008, after Iowa's production of 444.82 million bushels (12.11 million metric tons).[\[214\]](#page-38-10) Illinois ranks second in U. S. corn production with more than 1.5 billion bushels produced annually. [\[215\]](#page-38-11) With a production capacity of 1.5 billion gallons per year, Illinois is a top producer of ethanol, ranking third in the United States in 2011.[\[216\]](#page-38-12) Illinois is a leader in food manufacturing and meat processing.[\[217\]](#page-38-13) Although Chicago may no longer be ["Hog Butcher for the World"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_(poem)), the Chicago area remains a global center for [food manufacture and meat processing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_manufacturers_of_Chicago), [\[217\]](#page-38-13) with many plants, processing houses, and distribution facilities concentrated in the area of the former [Union Stock Yards.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Stock_Yards) [\[218\]](#page-38-14) Illinois also produces [wine,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_wine) and the state is home to two [American viticultural areas.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_viticultural_area) In the area of The Meeting of the Great Rivers Scenic Byway, peaches and apples are grown. The German immigrants from agricultural backgrounds who settled in Illinois in the mid- to late 19th century are in part responsible for the profusion of fruit orchards in that area of Illinois.[\[219\]](#page-38-15) Illinois's universities are actively researching alternative agricultural products as alternative crops.

#### **Manufacturing**

Illinois is one of the nation's manufacturing leaders, boasting annual value added productivity by manufacturing of over \$107 billion in 2006. As of 2011, Illinois is ranked as the 4th-most productive manufacturing state in the country, behind California, Texas, and Ohio.[\[220\]](#page-38-16) About three-quarters of the state's manufacturers are located in the Northeastern Opportunity Return Region, with 38 percent of Illinois's approximately 18,900 manufacturing plants located in Cook County. As of 2006, the leading manufacturing industries in Illinois, based upon value-added,

#### **Services**

By the early 2000s, Illinois's economy had moved toward a dependence on high-value-added services, such as financial trading, higher education, law, logistics, and medicine. In some cases, these services clustered around institutions that hearkened back to Illinois's earlier economies. For example, the [Chicago Mercantile Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Mercantile_Exchange), a trading exchange for global [derivatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derivative_(finance)), had begun its life as an agricultural [futures market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futures_exchange). Other important non-manufacturing industries include publishing, tourism, and energy production and distribution.

The Federal Reserve Bank of [Chicago,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank_of_Chicago) one of twelve Federal [Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank) Banks, at the heart of Chicago's financial center.

#### **Investments**

Venture capitalists funded a total of approximately \$62 billion in the U. S. economy in 2016. Of this amount, Illinois-based companies received approximately \$1.1 billion. Similarly, in FY 2016, the federal government spent \$461 billion on contracts in the U. S. Of this amount, Illinoisbased companies received approximately \$8.7 billion.

#### **Energy**

Illinois is a net importer of fuels for energy, despite large coal resources and some minor oil production. Illinois exports electricity, ranking fifth among states in electricity production and seventh in electricity consumption.[\[222\]](#page-38-18)

#### **Coal**

The coal industry of Illinois has its origins in the middle 19th century, when entrepreneurs such as [Jacob Loose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Loose) discovered coal in locations such as [Sangamon County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangamon_County). [Jacob Bunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Bunn) contributed to the development of the Illinois coal industry. Coal mining in central and southern Illinois was important in the late 19th and early 20th centuries. The [1909 Cherry Mine disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1909_Cherry_Mine_disaster) was an underground fire that killed 259 miners in the third worst disaster in the history of American coal mining. After 1940 there was a slow decline in coal mining.[\[223\]](#page-38-19) About 68% of Illinois has coal-bearing strata of the [Pennsylvanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvanian_(geology)) geologic period. According to the Illinois State Geological Survey, 211 billion tons of [bituminous coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bituminous_coal) are estimated to lie under the surface, having a total heating value greater than the estimated oil deposits in the [Arabian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Peninsula) [Peninsula.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Peninsula) [\[224\]](#page-38-20) However, this coal has a high [sulfur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfur) content, which causes [acid rain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_rain), unless special equipment is used to reduce [sulfur dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfur_dioxide) [emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_pollution). [\[26\]](#page-30-14)[\[29\]](#page-30-16)[\[68\]](#page-32-13) Many Illinois [power plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_station) are not equipped to burn high-sulfur coal. In 1999, Illinois produced 40.4 million tons of coal, but only 17 million tons (42%) of Illinois coal was consumed in Illinois. Most of the coal produced in Illinois is exported to other states and countries. In 2008, Illinois exported three million tons of coal and was projected to export nine million in 2011, as demand for energy grows in places such as China, India, and elsewhere in Asia and Europe. [225] As of 2010, Illinois was ranked third in recoverable coal reserves at producing mines in the nation. [216] Most of the coal produced in Illinois is exported to other states, while much of the coal burned for power in Illinois (21 million tons in 1998) is mined in the Powder River Basin of Wyoming. [222]

Mattoon was chosen as the site for the Department of Energy's Future Gen project, a 275-megawatt experimental zero emission coal-burning power plant that the DOE just gave a second round of funding. In 2010, after a number of setbacks, the city of Mattoon backed out of the project. [226]

#### Petroleum

Illinois is a leading refiner of petroleum in the American $\underline{\text{Midwest}}$ , with a combined crude oil distillation capacity of nearly 900,000 bbl/d (140,000 m³/d). However, Illinois has very limited crude oil proved reserves that account for less than 1% of the U. S. total reserves. Residential heating is 81% natural gas compared to less than 1% heating oil. Illinois is ranked 14th in oil production among states, with a daily output of approximately 28,000 bbl (4,500 m³) in $2005.\frac{[227][228]}{}$

## Location of the Illinois Basin

#### **Nuclear power**

Worldwide nuclear power began in Illinois during 1942 with the Chicago Pile-1, the world's first artificial self-sustaining nuclear chain reaction in the world's first nuclear reactor, built on the University of Chicago campus. There are six operating nuclear power plants in Illinois: Braidwood, Byron, Clinton, Dresden, La Salle, and Quad Cities. [229] With the exception of the single-unit Clinton plant, each of these facilities has two reactors. Three reactors have been permanently shut down and are in various stages of decommissioning: Dresden-1 and Zion-1 and 2. Illinois ranked first in the nation in 2010 in both nuclear capacity and nuclear generation. Generation from its nuclear power plants accounted for 12 percent of the nation's total. [216] In 2007, 48% of Illinois's electricity was generated using nuclear power. [230] The Morris Operation is the only de facto high-level radioactive waste storage site in the United States.

## ![](_page_20_Picture_8.jpeg)

## Byron Nuclear Generating Station in Ogle County

#### Wind power

Illinois has seen growing interest in the use of wind power for electrical generation. [231] Most of Illinois was rated in 2009 as "marginal or fair" for wind energy production by the U. S. Department of Energy, with some western sections rated "good" and parts of the south rated "poor". [232] These ratings are for wind turbines with 50 m (160 ft) hub heights; newer wind turbines are taller, enabling them to reach stronger winds farther from the ground. As a result, more areas of Illinois have become prospective wind farm sites. As of September 2009, Illinois had 1116.06 MW of installed wind power nameplate capacity with another 741.9 MW under construction. [233] Illinois ranked ninth among U. S. states in installed wind power capacity and sixteenth by potential capacity. [233] Large wind farms in Illinois include Twin Groves, Rail Splitter, Eco Grove, and Mendota Hills. [233]

As of 2007, wind energy represented only 1.7% of Illinois's energy production, and it was estimated that wind power could provide 5–10% of the state's energy needs. [234][235] Also, the Illinois General Assembly mandated in 2007 that by 2025, 25% of all electricity generated in Illinois is to come from renewable resources. [236]

#### **Biofuels**

Illinois is ranked second in $\underline{\text{corn}}$ production among U. S. states, and Illinois corn is used to produce 40% of the $\underline{\text{ethanol}}$ consumed in the United States. The $\underline{\text{Archer Daniels Midland}}$ corporation in Decatur, Illinois, is the world's leading producer of ethanol from corn.

The National Corn-to-Ethanol Research Center (NCERC), the world's only facility dedicated to researching the ways and means of converting corn (maize) to ethanol is located on the campus of Southern Illinois University Edwardsville. [237][238]

University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign is one of the partners in the Energy Biosciences Institute (EBI), a \$500 million biofuels research project funded by petroleum giant $\underline{BP}$ .

#### **Taxes**

Tax is collected by the [Illinois Department of Revenue.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Department_of_Revenue) State [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax) is calculated by multiplying [net income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_income) by a [flat rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_fee). In 1990, that rate was set at 3%, but in 2010, the General Assembly voted for a temporary increase in the rate to 5%; the new rate went into effect on January 1, 2011; the personal income rate partially sunset on January 1, 2015, to 3.75%, while the corporate income tax fell to 5.25%.[\[241\]](#page-39-16)[\[242\]](#page-39-17) Illinois failed to pass a budget from 2015 to 2017, after the 736-day [budget impasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Budget_Impasse), a budget was passed in Illinois after lawmakers overturned Governor Bruce Rauner's veto; this budget raised the personal income rate to 4.95% and the corporate rate to 7%.[\[243\]](#page-39-18) There are two rates for state [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax): 6.25% for general merchandise and 1% for qualifying food, drugs, and medical appliances.[\[244\]](#page-39-19) The [property tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_tax) is a major source of tax revenue for local government taxing districts. The property tax is a local—not state—tax imposed by local government taxing districts, which include counties, [townships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_township), municipalities, [school districts,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_district) and special taxation districts. The property tax in Illinois is imposed only on [real property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_property). [\[26\]](#page-30-14)[\[29\]](#page-30-16)[\[68\]](#page-32-13)

On May 1, 2019, the Illinois Senate voted to approve a [constitutional amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Fair_Tax) that would have stricken language from the [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Illinois) [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Illinois) requiring a flat state income tax, in a 73–44 vote. If approved, the amendment would have allowed the state legislature to impose a graduated income tax based on annual income. The governor, [J. B. Pritzker,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._B._Pritzker) approved the bill on May 27, 2019. It was scheduled for a 2020 general election ballot vote[\[245\]](#page-40-0)[\[246\]](#page-40-1) and required 60 percent voter approval to effectively amend the state constitution.[\[247\]](#page-40-2) The amendment was not approved by Illinoisans, with 55.1% of voters voting "No" on approval and 44.9% voting "Yes".[\[248\]](#page-40-3)

As of 2017 Chicago had the highest state and local sales tax rate for a U. S. city with a populations above 200,000, at 10.250%.[\[249\]](#page-40-4) The state of Illinois has the second highest rate of real estate tax: 2.31%, which is second only to New Jersey at 2.44%.[\[250\]](#page-40-5)

[Toll roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toll_road) are a *de facto* user tax on the citizens and visitors to the state of Illinois. Illinois ranks seventh out of the 11 states with the most miles of toll roads, at 282.1 miles. Chicago ranks fourth in most expensive toll roads in America by the mile, with the [Chicago Skyway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Skyway) charging 51.2 cents per mile.[\[251\]](#page-40-6) Illinois also has the 11th highest gasoline tax by state, at 37.5 cents per gallon.[\[252\]](#page-40-7)

# **Culture**

#### **Museums**

Illinois has numerous museums; the greatest concentration of these are in Chicago. Several museums in Chicago are ranked as some of the best in the world. These include the [John G. Shedd Aquarium,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shedd_Aquarium) the [Field Museum of Natural History,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_Museum_of_Natural_History) the [Art Institute of Chicago,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Institute_of_Chicago) the [Adler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adler_Planetarium) [Planetarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adler_Planetarium), and the [Museum of Science and Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Science_and_Industry_(Chicago)).

The modern [Abraham Lincoln Presidential Library and Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln_Presidential_Library_and_Museum) in Springfield is the largest and most attended [presidential library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_library_system) in the country. The Illinois State Museum boasts a collection of 13.5 million objects that tell the story of Illinois life, land, people, and art. The ISM is among only 5% of the nation's museums that are accredited by the American Alliance of Museums. Other historical museums in the state include the [Polish Museum of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Museum_of_America) in [Chicago;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago) [Magnolia Manor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnolia_Manor_(Cairo,_Illinois)) in [Cairo;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo,_Illinois) [Easley Pioneer Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easley_Pioneer_Museum) in [Ipava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipava,_Illinois); the [Elihu Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elihu_Benjamin_Washburne_House) [Washburne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elihu_Benjamin_Washburne_House); [Ulysses S. Grant Homes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant_Home), both in [Galena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galena,_Illinois); and the Chanute Air Museum, located on the former Chanute Air Force Base in Rantoul.

The Chicago metropolitan area also hosts two zoos: The [Brookfield Zoo,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookfield_Zoo) located about ten miles west of the city center in suburban [Brookfield,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookfield,_Illinois) contains more than 2,300 animals and covers 216 acres (87 ha). [Lincoln Park Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Park_Zoo) is located in [Lincoln Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Park) on Chicago's North Side, approximately 3 miles (4.8 km) north of the [Loop.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Chicago) The zoo, including [Nature Boardwalk,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_Boardwalk) occupies 49 acres (20 ha) of the park.

# **Music**

Illinois is a leader in music education, having hosted the [Midwest Clinic International Band and Orchestra Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwest_Clinic) since 1946, as well being home to the Illinois Music Educators Association (ILMEA, formerly IMEA), one of the largest professional music educator's organizations in the country. Each summer since 2004, [Southern Illinois University Carbondale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois_University_Carbondale) has played host to the Southern Illinois Music Festival, which presents dozens of performances throughout the region. Past featured artists include the [Eroica Trio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eroica_Trio) and violinist [David Kim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Kim_(violinist)).

[Chicago,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago) in the northeast corner of the state, is a [major center for music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Chicago)[\[253\]](#page-40-8) in the [midwestern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States) where distinctive forms of blues (greatly responsible for the future creation of [rock and roll\)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll), and [house music,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_music) a genre of electronic dance music, were developed.

The [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_(African_American)) of poor black workers from the South into the industrial cities brought traditional [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) and [blues music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues_music) to the city, resulting in [Chicago blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_blues) and "Chicago-style" [Dixieland jazz.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dixieland_jazz) Notable blues artists included [Muddy Waters,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muddy_Waters) [Junior Wells,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junior_Wells) [Howlin' Wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howlin%27_Wolf) and both [Sonny Boy Williamsons;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonny_Boy_Williamson_II) jazz greats included [Nat King Cole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nat_King_Cole), [Gene Ammons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_Ammons), [Benny Goodman,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benny_Goodman) and [Bud Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bud_Freeman). Chicago is also well known for its [soul music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music).

In the early 1930s, [Gospel music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_music) began to gain popularity in Chicago due to [Thomas A. Dorsey'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_A._Dorsey)s contributions at [Pilgrim Baptist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilgrim_Baptist_Church).

In the 1980s and 1990s, [heavy rock,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_rock) [punk,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_rock) and [hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop_music) also became popular in Chicago. [Orchestras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchestra) in Chicago include the [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Symphony_Orchestra) [Symphony Orchestra,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Symphony_Orchestra) the [Lyric Opera of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyric_Opera_of_Chicago), and the [Chicago Sinfonietta.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Sinfonietta) [\[254\]](#page-40-9)

#### **Movies**

[John Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hughes_(filmmaker)), who moved from [Grosse Pointe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grosse_Pointe,_Michigan) to [Northbrook,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northbrook,_Illinois) based many films of his in [Chicago,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago,_Illinois) and its suburbs. [Ferris Bueller's Day Off](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferris_Bueller%27s_Day_Off), [Home Alone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_Alone), [The Breakfast Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Breakfast_Club), and all his films take place in the fictional Shermer, Illinois (the original name of Northbrook was Shermerville, and Hughes's High School, [Glenbrook North High School,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenbrook_North_High_School) is on Shermer Road). Most locations in his films include Glenbrook North, the former [Maine North High School,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_North_High_School) the [Ben Rose House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Rose_House) in Highland Park, and the famous Home Alone house in [Winnetka,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winnetka,_Illinois) [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winnetka,_Illinois).

#### **Recreation**

The [Illinois state parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_state_parks) system began in 1908 with what is now [Fort Massac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Massac) State Park, becoming the first park in a system encompassing more than 60 parks and about the same number of recreational and wildlife areas.

Areas under the protection of the [National Park Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Park_Service) include: the [Illinois and Michigan Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_and_Michigan_Canal) [National Heritage Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_and_Michigan_Canal) near [Lockport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockport,_Illinois), [\[255\]](#page-40-10) the [Lewis and Clark National Historic Trail,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_and_Clark_National_Historic_Trail) the [Lincoln Home National Historic Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Home_National_Historic_Site) in Springfield, the [Mormon Pioneer National Historic Trail,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormon_Trail) the [Trail of Tears National Historic Trail,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trail_of_Tears) the [American Discovery Trail,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Discovery_Trail) [\[256\]](#page-40-11) the [Pullman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pullman_National_Monument) [National Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pullman_National_Monument), and [New Philadelphia Town Site.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Philadelphia_Town_Site) The federal government also manages the [Shawnee National Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawnee_National_Forest) and the [Midewin National Tallgrass Prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midewin_National_Tallgrass_Prairie).

## ![](_page_22_Picture_9.jpeg)

Sunset on the [Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_of_the_Gods_Wilderness) of the Gods [Wilderness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_of_the_Gods_Wilderness), part of the [Shawnee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawnee_National_Forest) National [Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawnee_National_Forest) in southern Illinois.

#### **Sports**

As one of the United States' major metropolises, all [major sports leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_professional_sports_leagues_in_the_United_States_and_Canada) have teams headquartered in Chicago.

Two [Major League Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Baseball) teams are located in the state. The [Chicago Cubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs) of the [National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_League_(baseball)) [League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_League_(baseball)) play in the second-oldest major league stadium, [Wrigley Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrigley_Field), and went the longest length of time without a championship in all of major American sport, from 1908 to 2016, when they won the [World Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Series). [\[257\]](#page-40-12)[\[258\]](#page-40-13) The [Chicago White Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_White_Sox) of the [American League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_League) won the [World Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Series) in 2005, their first since 1917. They play on the city's south side at [Rate Field.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rate_Field) The [Chicago Bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Bears) football team has won nine total NFL [Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_NFL_champions), the last occurring in [Super](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XX) [Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XX) XX on January 26, 1986. The [Chicago Bulls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Bulls) of the [NBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Basketball_Association) is one of the most recognized [basketball teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball) in the world, largely as a result of the efforts of [Michael Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jordan), who led the team to six NBA championships in eight seasons in the 1990s. The [Chicago Blackhawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Blackhawks) of the [NHL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Hockey_League) began playing in [1926](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1926%E2%80%9327_NHL_season) and became a member of the [Original Six](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_Six) once the NHL dropped to that number of teams during World War II. The Blackhawks have won six [Stanley Cups,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Cup) most recently in [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%9315_NHL_season). [Chicago Fire FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Fire_FC) is a member of [MLS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Soccer) and has been one of the league's most successful and best-supported clubs since its founding in 1997, winning one league and four [Lamar Hunt U. S. Open Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamar_Hunt_U. S._Open_Cup) in that timespan. The team played in [Bridgeview,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridgeview,_Illinois) adjacent to Chicago from 2006 to 2019. The team now plays at Soldier Field in Chicago.

The [Chicago Red Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Red_Stars) have played at the top level of U. S. women's soccer since their formation in 2009, except in the 2011 season. The team currently plays in the [National Women's Soccer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women%27s_Soccer_League) [League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women%27s_Soccer_League), playing at [Seat Geek Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seat Geek_Stadium), the Bridgeview venue it formerly shared with Fire FC. The [Chicago Sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Sky) have played in the [Women's National Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_National_Basketball_Association) (WNBA) since 2006. The Sky won their first WNBA Championship in 2021. They play at [Wintrust Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wintrust_Arena) in Chicago. The [Chicago Bandits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Bandits) of the [NPF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Pro_Fastpitch), a women's softball league, have won four league

[Soldier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soldier_Field) Field is home to the [National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League) [Football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League) League's [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Bears) Bears

[Wrigley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrigley_Field) Field is home to Major [League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Baseball) [Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Baseball)'s [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Cubs) Cubs titles, most recently in 2016. They play at [Parkway Bank Sports Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parkway_Bank_Sports_Complex) in [Rosemont, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosemont,_Illinois), in the Chicago area.

Many [minor league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_league) teams also call Illinois their home. They include the [Bloomington Edge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomington_Edge) of the [Indoor Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indoor_Football_League), [Bloomington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomington_Flex) [Flex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomington_Flex) of the [Midwest Professional Basketball Association,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwest_Professional_Basketball_Association) [Chicago Dogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Dogs) of the [American Association of Professional II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Fire_FC_II) of [MLS Next Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLS_Next_Pro), [Chicago Wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Wolves) of the [American Chiefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoria_Chiefs) of the [Midwest League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwest_League), [Peoria Rivermen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoria_Rivermen_(SPHL)) of the [SPHL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Professional_Hockey_League), [Rockford Aviators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockford_Aviators) of the Frontier League, [Rockford Ice Hogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockford_Ice Hogs) of the AHL, [Schaumburg Boomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schaumburg_Boomers) of the Frontier League, [Southern Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois_Miners) [Miners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois_Miners) in the Frontier League, [Windy City Bulls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windy_City_Bulls) of the [NBA G League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_G_League), and [Windy City Thunder Bolts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windy_City_Thunder Bolts) of the Frontier League.

The state features 13 athletic programs that compete in NCAA [Division I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I), the highest level of U. S. college sports. The two most prominent are the [Illinois Fighting Illini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Fighting_Illini) and [Northwestern Wildcats,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Wildcats) both members of the [Big Ten Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Ten_Conference) and the only ones competing in the "[Power Five conferences"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_Five_conferences). The Fighting Illini football team has won five national championships and three [Rose Bowl Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Bowl_Game), whereas the men's basketball team has won 17 conference seasons and played five Final Fours. Meanwhile, the Wildcats have won eight football conference championships and one Rose Bowl Game. The [Northern Illinois Huskies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Illinois_Huskies) compete in the [Mid-American Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-American_Conference), having won four conference championships and earning a bid in the [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Bowl). Four schools have football programs that compete in the second level of Division I football, the [Football Championship Subdivision.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_Championship_Subdivision) The [Illinois State Redbirds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_State_Redbirds) and [Southern Illinois Salukis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois_Salukis) are members of the [Missouri Valley Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Valley_Conference) for non-football sports and the [Missouri Valley Football Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Valley_Football_Conference). The [Eastern Illinois Panthers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Illinois_Panthers) and [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Illinois_Leathernecks) [Illinois Leathernecks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Illinois_Leathernecks) are members of the [Ohio Valley Conference.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_Valley_Conference) The city of Chicago is home to four Division I programs that do not sponsor football: the [De Paul Blue Demons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De Paul_Blue_Demons) of the [Big East Conference,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_East_Conference) [Loyola Ramblers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyola_Ramblers) of the [Atlantic 10 Conference,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_10_Conference) [UIC Flames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UIC_Flames) of the Missouri Valley Conference, and [Chicago State Cougars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_State_Cougars) of the [Northeast Conference.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Conference) Finally, two non-football Division I programs are located downstate. The [Bradley Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradley_Braves) are Missouri Valley Conference members, and the [SIU Edwardsville Cougars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SIU_Edwardsville_Cougars) compete in the Ohio Valley Conference.

[Motor racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_racing) oval tracks at the [Chicagoland Speedway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicagoland_Speedway) in [Joliet,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joliet,_Illinois) the [Chicago Motor Speedway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Motor_Speedway) in [Cicero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cicero,_Illinois) and the [Gateway Motorsports Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gateway_Motorsports_Park) in [Madison,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison,_Illinois) near St. Louis, have hosted [NASCAR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASCAR), [CART,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champ_Car) and [IRL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indy_Racing_League) races, whereas the [Sports Car Club of America,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Car_Club_of_America) among other national and regional [road racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_racing) clubs, have visited the [Autobahn Country Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autobahn_Country_Club) in Joliet, the [Blackhawk Farms Raceway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackhawk_Farms_Raceway) in [South Beloit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Beloit,_Illinois) and the former [Meadowdale International Raceway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meadowdale_International_Raceway) in [Carpentersville.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpentersville,_Illinois) Illinois also has several [short tracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_track_motor_racing) and [dragstrips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragstrip). The dragstrip at Gateway International Raceway and the [Route 66 Raceway,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Route_66_Raceway) which sits on the same property as the Chicagoland Speedway, both host [NHRA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHRA) drag races.

Illinois features several golf courses, such as [Olympia Fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia_Fields_Country_Club), [Medinah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medinah_Country_Club), [Midlothian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midlothian_Country_Club), [Cog Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cog_Hill_Golf_%26_Country_Club), and [Conway Farms,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conway_Farms_Golf_Club) which have often hosted the BMW [Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMW_Championship_(PGA_Tour)), [Western Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Open), and [Women's Western Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_Western_Open). Also, the state has hosted 13 editions of the [U. S. Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U. S._Open_(golf)) (latest at Olympia Fields in 2003), six editions of the PGA [Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGA_Championship) (latest at Medinah in 2006), three editions of the [U. S. Women's Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U. S._Women%27s_Open) (latest at The Merit Club), the [2009 Solheim Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Solheim_Cup) (at Rich Harvest Farms), and the [2012 Ryder Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Ryder_Cup) (at Medinah). The [John Deere Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Deere_Classic) is a regular [PGA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGA_Tour) Tour event played in the Quad Cities since 1971, whereas the [Encompass Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encompass_Championship) is a [Champions Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champions_Tour) event since 2013. Previously, the [LPGA State Farm Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LPGA_State_Farm_Classic) was an [LPGA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LPGA_Tour) Tour event from 1976 to 2011.

# **Law and politics**

In a 2020 study, Illinois was ranked as the 4th easiest state for citizens to vote in.[\[259\]](#page-40-14)

#### **State government**

The [government of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Illinois), under the [Constitution of Illinois,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Illinois) has three branches of government: executive, legislative, and judicial. The executive branch is split into several statewide elected offices, with the governor as chief executive. Legislative functions are granted to the Illinois General Assembly. The judiciary is composed of the Supreme Court and lower courts.

The executive branch is composed of six elected officers and their offices as well as numerous other departments.[\[260\]](#page-40-15) The six elected officers are:[\[260\]](#page-40-15) [Governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Illinois), [Lieutenant Governor,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Illinois) [Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Attorney_General), [Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Secretary_of_State), [Comptroller,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Comptroller) and [Treasurer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Treasurer). The government of Illinois has numerous departments, agencies, boards and commissions, but the so-called code departments provide most of the state's services.[\[260\]](#page-40-15)[\[261\]](#page-40-16)

## ![](_page_23_Picture_9.jpeg)

The Illinois State [Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_State_Capitol) in [Springfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield,_Illinois)

The [Illinois General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_General_Assembly) is the state legislature, composed of the 118-member [Illinois House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_House_of_Representatives) and the 59-member [Illinois Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Senate). The members of the General Assembly are elected at the beginning of each even-numbered year. The *[Illinois Compiled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Compiled_Statutes) [Statutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Compiled_Statutes)* (ILCS) are the [codified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codification_(law)) statutes of a general and permanent nature.[\[262\]](#page-40-17)[\[263\]](#page-40-18)

The [Judiciary of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_of_Illinois) is the unified court system of Illinois. It consists of the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Illinois), [Appellate Court,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Appellate_Court) and [Circuit Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Circuit_Courts). The Supreme Court oversees the administration of the court system.

The [administrative divisions of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Illinois) are counties, townships, precincts, cities, towns, villages, and special-purpose districts.[\[264\]](#page-40-19) The basic subdivision of Illinois are the 102 counties.[\[265\]](#page-40-20) Eighty-five of the 102 counties are in turn divided into townships and precincts.[\[265\]](#page-40-20)[\[266\]](#page-41-0) Municipal governments are the cities, villages, and incorporated towns.[\[265\]](#page-40-20) Some localities possess [home rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_rule), which allows them to govern themselves to a certain extent.[\[267\]](#page-41-1)

#### Party balance

In modern national and state politics, Illinois is a Democratic stronghold. [268] Historically, Illinois was a political swing state, with near-parity existing between the Republican and the Democratic parties. However, in recent elections, the Democratic Party has gained ground, and Illinois has come to be seen as a solid "blue" state in both presidential and congressional campaigns. [269][270] Illinois's Democratic tendencies are mostly attributable to Cook County and Chicago, by far the state's largest county and city, respectively, which have long been strongly Democratic. The collar counties, affluent suburban counties that surround Cook County, historically voted Republican and helped keep the state competitive; however, they have swung toward the left in recent elections as the national Republican Party has become increasingly conservative, which has cemented Democratic dominance in state politics. [271] Outside of the

Chicago metropolitan area, the state's rural areas are heavily Republican. The dominance of the Chicago area in state elections is so overwhelming that it has influenced a secessionist movement in the downstate region. [272]

Illinois was long seen as a national bellwether, [273] supporting the winner in every election in the 20th century, except for 1916 and 1976. Since the 1992 election, however, Illinois has trended more toward the Democratic Party and is part of the "blue wall" of states that have consistently voted Democratic in the last six presidential elections. In 2000, George W. Bush became the first Republican to win the presidency without carrying either Illinois or Vermont, with Donald Trump repeating the feat in 2016. Illinois has not elected a Republican to the Senate since Mark Kirk won in 2010; the last Republicans to hold statewide office were Governor Bruce Rauner and Lieutenant Governor Evelyn Sanguinetti, who both left office in 2019.

## Governor J. B. Pritzker (D)

#### **History of corruption**

Politics in the state have been infamous for highly visible corruption cases, as well as for crusading reformers, such as governors Adlai Stevenson and James R. Thompson. In 2006, former governor George Ryan was convicted of racketeering and bribery, leading to a six-and-a-half-year prison sentence. On December 7, 2011, former governor Rod Blagojevich was sentenced to 14 years in prison for allegations that he conspired to sell the vacated Senate seat left by President Barack Obama to the highest bidder. Blagojevich had earlier been impeached and convicted by the legislature, resulting in his removal from office. In the late 20th century, Congressman Dan Rostenkowski was imprisoned for mail fraud; former governor and federal judge Otto Kerner Jr. was imprisoned for bribery; Secretary of State Paul Powell was investigated and found to have gained great wealth through bribes, and State Auditor of Public Accounts (Comptroller) Orville Hodge was imprisoned for embezzlement. In 1912, William Lorimer, the GOP boss of Chicago, was expelled from the U. S. Senate for bribery, and in 1921, Governor Len Small was found to have defrauded the state of a million dollars.

#### U. S. presidential elections

Illinois has shown a strong presence in presidential elections. Three presidents have claimed Illinois as their political base when running for president: Abraham Lincoln, Ulysses S. Grant, and most recently Barack Obama. Lincoln was born in Kentucky, but he moved to Illinois at age 21. He served in the General Assembly and represented the 7th congressional district in the U. S. House of Representatives before his election to the presidency in 1860. Ulysses S. Grant was born in Ohio and had a military career that precluded settling down, but on the eve of the Civil War and approaching middle age, he moved to Illinois and thus utilized the state as his home and political base when running for president. Barack Obama was born in Hawaii and made Illinois his home after graduating from law school, and later represented Illinois in the U. S. Senate. He then became president in 2008, running as a candidate from his Illinois base.

Ronald Reagan was born in Illinois, in the city of Tampico, raised in Dixon, Illinois, and educated at Eureka College, outside Peoria. Reagan later moved to California during his young adulthood. He then became an actor, and later became California's Governor before being elected president.

Hillary Clinton was born and raised in the suburbs of Chicago and became the first woman to represent a major political party in the general election of the U. S. presidency. Clinton ran from a platform based in New York State.

#### African-American U. S. senators

Twelve African-Americans have served as members of the United States Senate. Of which three have represented Illinois, the most of any single state: Carol Moseley-Braun, [129] Barack Obama, [275] and Roland Burris, who was appointed to replace Obama after his election to the presidency. Moseley-Braun was the first African-American woman to become a U. S. Senator.

#### **Political families**

Several families from Illinois have played particularly prominent roles in politics, in both the [Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)) earlier in the state's history but more recently the [Democratic Party,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)) gaining both statewide and national fame.

#### **Ingersoll**

The [Ingersoll family of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_political_families_(I)#The_Ingersolls_of_Illinois) comprised a pair of brothers who held several prominent elected positions representing Illinois.

- [Ebon C. Ingersoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebon_C._Ingersoll) (1831–1879), Illinois State Representative 1856, U. S. Representative from Illinois 1864–71. Brother of Robert G. Ingersoll.[\[276\]](#page-41-10)
- [Robert G. Ingersoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_G._Ingersoll) (1833–1899), Illinois State Representative 1860, Attorney General of Illinois 1867–69, delegate to the Republican National Convention 1876. Brother of Ebon C. Ingersoll.[\[276\]](#page-41-10)
 - John C. Ingersoll (1860–1903), U. S. Consul in [Cartagena, Colombia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartagena,_Colombia) 1902. Son of Ebon C. Ingersoll.[\[276\]](#page-41-10)

#### **Stevenson**

The [Stevenson family,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stevenson_family) initially rooted in central Illinois and later based in the Chicago metropolitan area, has provided four generations of Illinois officeholders.

- [Adlai Stevenson I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adlai_Stevenson_I) (1835–1914) was a Vice President of the United States, as well as a [Congressman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressman)
- [Lewis Stevenson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Stevenson_(politician)) (1868–1929), son of Adlai, served as [Illinois Secretary of State.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Secretary_of_State)
- [Adlai Stevenson II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adlai_Stevenson_II) (1900–1965), son of Lewis, served as [Governor of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Illinois) and as the U. S. Ambassador to the United Nations; he was also the Democratic party's presidential nominee in [1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_United_States_presidential_election) and [1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956_United_States_presidential_election), losing both elections to [Dwight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_Eisenhower) [Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_Eisenhower).
- [Adlai Stevenson III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adlai_Stevenson_III) (1930–2021), son of Adlai II, served ten years as a [United States Senator.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senator)

#### **Daley**

The [Daley family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daley_family)'s powerbase was in Chicago.

- [Richard J. Daley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_J._Daley) (1902–1976) served as [Mayor of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Chicago) from 1955 to his death.
- [Richard M. Daley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_M._Daley) (born 1942), son of Richard J, was Chicago's longest-serving mayor, in office from 1989 to 2011.
- [William M. Daley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_M._Daley) (born 1948), another son of Richard J, is a former [White House Chief of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Chief_of_Staff) and has served in a variety of appointed positions.

#### **Pritzker**

The [Pritzker family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pritzker_family) is based in Chicago and have played important roles in both the private and the public sectors.

- [Jay Pritzker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Pritzker) (1922–1999), co-founder of [Hyatt Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyatt_Hotel) based in Chicago.
- [Penny Pritzker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Pritzker) (born 1959), 38th [United States Secretary of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_Commerce) under President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama).
- [J. B. Pritzker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._B._Pritzker) (born 1965), current and 43rd [governor of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Illinois) and co-founder of the Pritzker Group.

#### **Madigan**

Members of the [Madigan family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Madigan#The_Madigan_family_and_their_role_in_Illinois_government) have held extensive influence in Illinois politics.

- [Michael Madigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Madigan) (born 1942), longtime speaker of the [Illinois House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_House_of_Representatives) and notorious [political boss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_boss).
- [Lisa Madigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Madigan) (born 1966), adopted daughter of Michael Madigan, former [Illinois Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Attorney_General).

# **Education**

#### **Illinois State Board of education**

The Illinois State Board of Education (ISBE) is autonomous of the governor and the state legislature, and administers [public education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_education) in the state. Local municipalities and their respective [school districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_district) operate individual public schools, but the ISBE audits performance of public schools with the [Illinois School Report Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_School_Report_Card). The ISBE also makes recommendations to state leaders concerning education spending and policies.

# **Primary and secondary schools**

Education is compulsory for ages 7–17 in Illinois. Schools are commonly, but not exclusively, divided into three tiers of primary and secondary education: elementary school, [middle school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_school) or [junior high school,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_school) and high school. District territories are often complex in structure. Many areas in the state are actually located in *two* school districts—one for high school and the other for elementary and middle schools. And such districts do not necessarily share boundaries. A given high school may have several elementary districts that feed into it, yet some of those feeder districts may themselves feed into multiple high school districts.

#### **Colleges and universities**

Using the criterion established by [the Carnegie Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Carnegie_Foundation_for_the_Advancement_of_Teaching), there are eleven "National Universities" in the state.

As of 19 August 2010, six of these rank in the "first tier" among the top 500 National Universities in the nation, as determined by the *[U. S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U. S._News_%26_World_Report)* rankings: the [University of Chicago,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Chicago) [Northwestern University,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_University) the [University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Illinois_Urbana-Champaign) [Loyola University Chicago,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyola_University_Chicago) the [Illinois Institute of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Institute_of_Technology), [De Paul University,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De Paul_University) [University of Illinois Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Illinois_Chicago), [Illinois State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_State_University) [University,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_State_University) [Southern Illinois University Carbondale,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois_University_Carbondale) and [Northern Illinois University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Illinois_University). [\[277\]](#page-41-11) The University of Chicago is continuously ranked as one of the world's top ten universities on various independent university rankings, and its [Booth School of Business,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Booth_School_of_Business) along with Northwestern's [Kellogg School of Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kellogg_School_of_Management) consistently rank within the top five graduate business schools in the

Campus of the [University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Chicago) of [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Chicago) Foellinger [Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foellinger_Auditorium) at the [University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Illinois_Urbana-Champaign) of Illinois Urbana-[Champaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Illinois_Urbana-Champaign)

country and top ten globally. The University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign is often ranked among the best engineering schools in the world and United States.

Illinois also has more than twenty additional [accredited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_accreditation) four-year universities, both public and private, and dozens of small [liberal arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_arts_college) [colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_arts_college) across the state. Additionally, Illinois supports 49 public [community colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_college) in the [Illinois Community College System.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Community_College_System)

#### **School financing**

Schools in Illinois are funded primarily by property taxes, based on state assessment of property values, rather than direct state contributions. Scholar Tracy Steffes has described Illinois public education as historically "inequitable", a system where one of "the wealthiest of states" is "the stingiest in its support for education". There have been several attempts to reform school funding in Illinois. The most notable attempt came in 1973 with the adoption of the Illinois Resource Equalizer Formula, a measure through which it was hoped funding could be collected and distributed to Illinois schools more equitably. However, opposition from affluent Illinois communities who objected to having to pay for the less well-off school districts (many of them Black majority communities, produced by redlining, white flight, and other "soft" segregation methods) resulted in the formula's abolition in the late 1980s.[\[278\]](#page-41-12)

# **Transportation**

Because of its central location and its proximity to the [Rust Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rust_Belt) and [Grain Belt,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain_Belt) Illinois is a national crossroads for air, auto, rail, and truck traffic.

#### **Airports**

From 1962 until 1998, Chicago's [O'Hare International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O%27Hare_International_Airport) (ORD) was the busiest airport in the world, measured both in terms of total flights and passengers. While it was surpassed by [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta)'s [Hartsfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartsfield%E2%80%93Jackson_Atlanta_International_Airport) in 1998 (as Chicago splits its air traffic between O'Hare and Midway airports, while Atlanta uses only one airport), with 59.3 million domestic passengers annually, along with 11.4 million international passengers in 2008,[\[279\]](#page-41-13) O'Hare consistently remains one of the two or three busiest airports globally, and in some years still ranks number one in total flights. It is a major [hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airline_hub) for both [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines) and [American Airlines,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines) and a major airport expansion project is currently underway. [Midway Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Midway_International_Airport) (MDW), which had been the busiest airport in the world at one point until it was supplanted by O'Hare as the busiest airport in 1962, is now the secondary airport in the Chicago metropolitan area and still ranks as one of the nation's busiest airports. Midway is a major hub for [Southwest Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Airlines) and services many other carriers as well. Midway served 17.3 million domestic and international passengers in 2008.[\[280\]](#page-41-14)

# **Highways**

The Interstate Highways in Illinois are all segments of the [Interstate Highway System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Highway_System) that are owned and maintained by the state.[\[281\]](#page-41-15) Major U. S. Interstate highways crossing the state include: [Interstate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_24_in_Illinois) 24 (I-24), [I-39](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_39_in_Illinois), [I-41,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_41) [I-55](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_55_in_Illinois), [I-57](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_57_in_Illinois), [I-64,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_64_in_Illinois) [I-70](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_70_in_Illinois), [I-72](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_72_in_Illinois), [I-74,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_74_in_Illinois) [I-80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_80_in_Illinois), [I-88](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_88_(Illinois)), [I-90,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_90_in_Illinois) and [I-94.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_94_in_Illinois)

Illinois has the distinction of having the most primary (two-digit) interstates pass through it among all the 50 states with 13. Illinois also ranks third among the fifty states with the most interstate mileage, coming in after California and Texas, which are much bigger states in area.[\[282\]](#page-41-16)

The [Illinois Department of Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Department_of_Transportation) (IDOT) is responsible for maintaining the [U. S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Numbered_Highways) [Highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Numbered_Highways) in Illinois. #### **Buses**

Due to its central location, Illinois sees numerous intercity bus services primarily connecting east and west. The [Chicago Bus Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Bus_Station) is the busiest intercity bus station in the state. The following carriers provide scheduled service: [Amtrak Thruway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak_Thruway), [Barons Bus Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barons_Bus_Lines), [Burlington Trailways,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burlington_Trailways) [Flixbus,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flixbus) [Greyhound Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greyhound_Lines), [Indian Trails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Trails), Miller Transportation (Hoosier Ride), [Peoria Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoria_Charter_Coach_Company) [Coach Company,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoria_Charter_Coach_Company) [Van Galder Bus Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Galder_Bus_Company), and [Wisconsin Coach Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Coach_Lines).

#### **Railroads**

Illinois has an extensive passenger and freight rail transportation network. Chicago is a national [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak) hub and in-state passengers are served by Amtrak's [Illinois Service,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Service) featuring the Chicago to Carbondale *[Illini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illini_(Amtrak))* and *[Saluki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saluki_(Amtrak))*, the Chicago to Quincy *[Carl Sandburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Zephyr_and_Carl_Sandburg)* and *[Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Zephyr_and_Carl_Sandburg) [Zephyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Zephyr_and_Carl_Sandburg)*, and the Chicago to St. Louis *[Lincoln Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Service)*. Currently there is trackwork on the Chicago–St. Louis line to bring the maximum speed up to 110 mph (180 km/h), which would reduce the trip time by an hour and a half. Nearly every North American railway meets at Chicago (including all six [Class I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railroad_classes) railroads), making it the largest and most active rail hub in the country. Extensive heavy rail service is provided in the city proper and some immediate suburbs by the [Chicago Transit Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Transit_Authority)'s ['L'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_%22L%22) system. One of the largest suburban commuter rail system in the United States, operated by [Metra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metra), uses existing rail lines to provide direct commuter rail access for hundreds of suburbs to the city and beyond.

#### **Waterways**

In addition to the state's rail lines, the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) and [Illinois River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_River) provide major transportation routes for the state's agricultural interests. [Lake Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan) gives Illinois access to the Atlantic Ocean by way of the [Saint Lawrence Seaway.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_Seaway)

Illinois borders the state of [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin) to its north, [Iowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iowa) to its northwest, [Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri) to its southwest, [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky) to its south, [Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana) to its east, and has a water border with [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan) to the northeast in [Lake Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan).
- c. Persons of Hispanic or Latino origin are not distinguished between total and partial ancestry.
- d. Estimate based on the white population "of Spanish mother tongue" in the 1940 Census. Prior to the introduction of 'some other race' as a census category, the vast majority of Hispanics were counted as white, but this estimate may still exclude some non-white Hispanics and those of Hispanic heritage who didn't speak Spanish.
- e. Arabs and other Middle Eastern Americans are classified as racially 'white' by the Census Bureau
